````json
  "content": "&lt;agent_context>\n项目：gen-depth-fingerprint。论文关于递归 LLM 生成链的深度指纹检测。7 features: entropy_mean, entropy_std, entropy_max, entropy_min, tail_decay (reference-model features) + ttr, hapax_ratio (surface features)。3-class depth classification: gen-0, gen-1, gen-2+。6 generators: pythia-2.8b, pythia-1b, olmo-2-1b, qwen3-0.6b, qwen3-1.7b, qwen3-8b (0.6B--8.2B range)。LOGO = Leave-One-Generator-Out。6-gen LOGO F1 = 0.789。Claim 3 (downstream utility) 是 negative result (D009)。参考模型: Pythia-410M。\n\n注意：参数范围写 0.6B--8.2B，不要改成 0.6B--8B。这是已确认的正确写法。\n&lt;/agent_context>\n\n&lt;project_context>\n# 项目: gen-depth-fingerprint\n\n## 运行环境\n- 服务器名: autodl-5090\n- SSH 账户: root（调用 ssh_execute / submit_training_job 时传入 account=\"root\"）\n- SSH 连接命令: ssh autodl-5090  # 必须用此命令，~/.ssh/config 自动应用密钥和跳板机，禁止用裸 IP\n- 项目代码根目录: /root/gen-depth-fingerprint\n\n\n## 环境搭建\n连接服务器后先调 `detect_image` 检测是否为预置镜像。如果匹配，跳过基础环境搭建，只装项目特有依赖。已预装的包不要重复安装。\n\n## 输出风格\n回复简洁直接。只输出执行结果和必要信息，不要解释过程、不要客套、不要给未被要求的建议。\n&lt;/project_context>\n## 任务：审稿修订写作修改（第一批，不依赖实验结果）\n\n**工作目录**: `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/`\n**论文文件**: `artifacts/_project/paper/` 目录下的 .tex 和 .bib 文件\n**同步目标**: 改完后同步到 `docs/paper/` 目录\n\n**重要**：每项改完后 git commit（一项一个 commit，便于回滚）。commit message 格式：`paper: W{N} — {简述}`\n\n先用 Read 工具读取所有需要修改的文件，理解当前内容，然后逐项修改。\n\n---\n\n### W2 — Cross-scale claim 加 caveat\n文件：`artifacts/_project/paper/results.tex`\n找到 \"Cross-scale transfer\" 相关段落末尾，加一句：\n> \"We note that this gap may narrow with scale-matched training data; our current dataset contains $\\sim$1{,}000 samples per generator regardless of scale.\"\n\n### W4 — 补充缺失引用 + 区分 source attribution vs depth estimation\n文件：`artifacts/_project/paper/related_work.tex` + `artifacts/_project/paper/references.bib`\n\n1. 在 `references.bib` 中加 4 篇引用（先检查是否已存在）：\n   - MR1: Uchendu et al. (2021) \"Through the Looking Glass: Learning to Attribute Synthetic Text Generated by Language Models\" (NAACL 2021 Findings) — 注意：如果搜不到精确信息，用合理的 bib 条目，但在 commit message 中标注\"需验证\"\n   - MR3: Mitchell et al. (2024) \"Spotting LLMs With Binoculars: Zero-Shot Detection of Machine-Generated Text\" (ICML 2024) — 先检查是否已引用\n\n2. 在 `related_work.tex` 中找 detection 文献讨论后加一段，明确区分：\n   - Source attribution: 识别哪个模型生成了文本（who generated it）\n   - Depth estimation: 识别文本经过了几代递归生成（how many recursive steps）\n   - 两者正交：同一个模型可以产生不同深度的文本\n   - 引用 MR1、MR2 作为 source attribution 代表工作\n\n### W5 — Contribution 3 降级\n文件：`artifacts/_project/paper/intro.tex`\n找到 contribution list（通常是 enumerate/itemize 环境），将第三个 contribution 改为：\n> \"As a complementary analysis, we show that depth-aware data curation does not improve downstream language model quality compared to random subsampling, establishing depth fingerprinting as a diagnostic rather than curative tool.\"\n\n同时检查 `artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex`（或 main.tex 中的 abstract）和 `artifacts/_project/paper/conclusion.tex`，如果也提到 \"third contribution\"，同样降级措辞。\n\n### W7 — entropy_min 重新分类\n文件：`artifacts/_project/paper/method.tex`\n1. 找 Surface features 段落，将 entropy_min 从 surface features 移到 reference-model features\n2. This two-pronged design is not post-hoc: it follows directly from the hypothesis that recursive generation induces a location shift in the NLL distribution (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\"\n\n### W10 — 3-class 合并理由\n文件：`artifacts/_project/paper/method.tex`\n找到 Task Formulation 段落中 \"preliminary experiments showed diminishing discriminability at higher depths\"，改为：\n> \"consistent with monotonic feature convergence that compresses inter-class margins beyond depth 2 (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential})\"\n\n如果找不到这段原文，在 task formulation 讨论 3-class 合并的地方加入类似理由。\n\n### W14 — Limitations section\n检查是否已有 Limitations section。如果没有，在 Conclusion 之前加：\n```latex\n\\subsection{Limitations}\nOur study has several limitations.\nFirst, we evaluate only continuation-style chains; paraphrase or instruction-following pipelines may exhibit different depth dynamics.\nSecond, the reference model is fixed to Pythia-410M throughout, although cross-family reference experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-ref}) suggest the phenomenon is not reference-specific.\nThird, all seed texts are English Wikipedia excerpts; generalization to other languages and domains remains untested.\nFourth, the cross-scale transfer gap (Table~\\ref{tab:logo}) is not fully closed, suggesting that scale-specific calibration may be needed for production deployment.\n```\n\n### W16 — XGBoost 超参数报告\n文件：`artifacts/_project/paper/method.tex`\n找到 Classifier 段落（讨论 XGBoost 的地方），加一句：\n> \"We use $n\\_\\text{estimators}=200$, $\\max\\_\\text{depth}=6$, and learning rate $0.1$ throughout all experiments, including baseline comparisons, to ensure a controlled comparison.\"\n\n---\n\n### 同步到 docs/paper/\n所有修改完成后，将 `artifacts/_project/paper/` 中修改过的文件复制到 `docs/paper/` 对应位置。如果 `docs/paper/` 目录不存在或结构不同，先 ls 查看。\n\n### 完成后\n列出每项修改的具体内容和 commit hash。如果某项需要信息你没有（如 W10 的 5-class 具体数字），在输出中标注\"缺失信息：XXX\"。\n"
````

### W14 — Limitations section
检查是否已有 Limitations section。如果没有，在 Conclusion 之前加：
```latex
\subsection{Limitations}
Our study has several limitations.
First, we evaluate only continuation-style chains; paraphrase or instruction-following pipelines may exhibit different depth dynamics.
Second, the reference model is fixed to Pythia-410M throughout, although cross-family reference experiments (\S\ref{sec:cross-ref}) suggest the phenomenon is not reference-specific.
Third, all seed texts are English Wikipedia excerpts; generalization to other languages and domains remains untested.
Fourth, the cross-scale transfer gap (Table~\ref{tab:logo}) is not fully closed, suggesting that scale-specific calibration may be needed for production deployment.
```

```text
Perfect! I have successfully read all the requested files. Here is the complete content of each file:

## 1. results.tex
The file contains detailed evaluation results across four axes:
- **Depth Classification Performance**: Reports leave-one-generator-out (LOGO) macro-F1 of 0.789 for 3-class depth estimation across six generators. Per-generator F1 ranges from 0.683 (Qwen3-8B) to 0.856 (Pythia-1B).
- **Differential Depth Response**: Central-tendency and diversity features show strong monotonic drift with depth, while shape features remain near-stationary.
- **Comparison with Detection Baselines**: The 7-feature ensemble outperforms all single-detector baselines (+3.5pp over Avg-LogProb, p<0.001).
- **Downstream Utility**: A negative finding showing that depth-aware data curation does not outperform random subsampling—data quantity dominates depth composition.

## 2. related_work.tex
Covers five main areas:
- **AI-Generated Text Detection**: Reviews evolution from statistical tests to zero-shot and learned approaches
- **Model Collapse and Iterative Generation**: Discusses training-loop vs. inference-chain degradation
- **Vocabulary Diversity and Stylometry**: Documents declining lexical diversity in LLM text
- **Data Quality and Provenance**: Covers curation methods and watermarking approaches
- The paper positions depth fingerprinting as complementary to existing detection and curation methods

## 3. references.bib
Contains 68 bibliographic entries organized by categories:
- Detection methods (DetectGPT, GLTR, Binoculars, etc.)
- Model collapse literature
- OOD detection, data quality
- Watermarking, vocabulary diversity
- Stylometry, iterative generation
- Membership inference
- Model citations (Pythia, OLMo, Qwen3)
- XGBoost for machine learning

## 4. intro.tex
The introduction file is NOT present (file does not exist error). The main.tex references `\input{introduction}` but the actual file is named differently.

## 5. method.tex
Details the methodology with four subsections:
- **Task Formulation**: Ordinal classification over three depth classes (gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+)
- **Generation Chain Construction**: Uses 6 generators from Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen families (0.6B-8.2B parameters)
- **Feature Extraction**: 7 features total—4 reference-model features (entropy_mean, entropy_std, entropy_max, tail_decay) and 3 surface features (TTR, hapax_ratio, entropy_min)
- **Evaluation Protocol**: LOGO cross-validation, XGBoost classifier, macro-F1 metric, statistical testing

## 6. conclusion.tex
Brief conclusion summarizing:
- Ordinal depth estimation as a task beyond binary detection
- 7-feature ensemble achieving 0.79 macro-F1
- Differential depth response pattern documented
- Depth fingerprinting positioned as diagnostic, not curative
- Future work suggestions for frontier-scale generators, mixed-generator chains, and multilingual settings

## 7. abstract.tex
Contains the paper's abstract describing:
- The core problem: recursively generated text of unknown depth in training corpora
- The solution: ordinal depth estimation using a 7-feature ensemble
- Key result: 0.79 macro-F1 with tight cross-generator consistency (1.4pp CI vs 18-26pp for baselines)
- Important negative finding: depth-aware curation doesn't outperform random subsampling

## 8. main.tex
LaTeX document structure file that:
- Uses ACL 2023 template
- Includes documentclass, packages, and graphics configuration
- References input files: introduction, related_work, method, results, discussion, conclusion, limitations, ethics
- Includes 6 appendices on length, prompt type, finer-grained depth, LOFO, confusion matrix, and classifier sensitivity
- Points to references.bib for bibliography

## docs/paper/ directory
The directory exists and contains 26 files including:
- All the tex files mentioned above
- Generated LaTeX artifacts (main.aux, main.bbl, main.log, main.out)
- Compiled PDF (main.pdf, 320KB)
- ACL stylesheet files (acl.sty, acl_natbib.bst)
- A _pdf_images subdirectory for figures
- A symbolic link to the figures directory

**Note**: The "intro.tex" file mentioned in the original list does not exist. The main.tex references `\input{introduction}` but the file is not present in the paper directory. All other requested files are available and have been read in their entirety.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01ADn4MTiPfceugy7uFfwjNH`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We evaluate depth fingerprinting along four axes: cross-generator classification (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}), differential depth response of individual features (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}), comparison with existing detection baselines (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}), and downstream utility for data curation (\S\ref{sec:results-downstream}).
5	
6	\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}
7	\label{sec:results-classification}
8	
9	Table~\ref{tab:logo-results} reports leave-one-generator-out (LOGO) macro-F1 for 3-class depth estimation (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+) across six generators spanning three model families and parameter counts from 0.6B to 8.2B.
10	
11	The 7-feature ensemble achieves LOGO F1\,=\,0.789, significantly outperforming all single-detector baselines ($p<0.001$; \S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
12	Per-generator held-out F1 ranges from 0.683 (Qwen3-8B) to 0.856 (Pythia-1B).
13	Per-class F1 varies by depth: gen-0\,=\,0.94, gen-2+\,=\,0.80, gen-1\,=\,0.64; the primary difficulty lies in distinguishing gen-1 from gen-2+, where feature margins narrow.
14	
15	\paragraph{Cross-scale transfer.}
16	The six-generator evaluation includes Qwen3-8B (8.2B parameters), confirming that depth signal persists at scales beyond the $\leq$2.8B training generators.
17	However, cross-scale transfer incurs a moderate gap: Qwen3-8B held-out F1\,=\,0.683 (CI [0.668, 0.694]), compared to 0.76--0.86 for $\leq$2.8B generators.
18	Text generated by larger models is more fluent, compressing feature margins and making depth distinctions harder.
19	Notably, adding Qwen3-8B data \emph{improves} classification on the original five generators (+1.1\,pp to 0.811), indicating that cross-scale diversity benefits rather than dilutes the training signal.
20	
21	\paragraph{Cross-family generalization.}
22	Leave-one-family-out (LOFO) evaluation---holding out an entire model family and training on the remaining two---yields macro-F1 of 0.772 (Pythia held out), 0.762 (OLMo held out), and 0.754 (Qwen held out), confirming that depth features transfer across architectural families with only modest degradation (average $\Delta$\,=\,$-$2.6\,pp vs.\ LOGO).
23	
24	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
25	Text length correlates weakly with depth (Spearman $|\rho| < 0.14$).
26	Length-normalized feature trends are preserved, and length-matched subsampling yields comparable or higher F1 ($\Delta$\,=\,+0.041), ruling out length as a confound (Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
27	
28	
29	\begin{table}[t]
30	\centering
31	\small
32	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
33	\toprule
34	\textbf{Generator} & \textbf{Family} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{Held-out F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
35	\midrule
36	Pythia-1B       & Pythia & 1.0B & 0.856 & [0.843, 0.863] \\
37	Pythia-2.8B     & Pythia & 2.8B & 0.804 & [0.793, 0.813] \\
38	OLMo-2-1B      & OLMo   & 1.5B & 0.764 & [0.754, 0.776] \\
39	Qwen3-0.6B     & Qwen   & 0.6B & 0.824 & [0.813, 0.834] \\
40	Qwen3-1.7B     & Qwen   & 1.7B & 0.805 & [0.796, 0.817] \\
41	Qwen3-8B       & Qwen   & 8.2B & 0.683 & [0.668, 0.694] \\
42	\midrule
43	\multicolumn{3}{l}{Average (6-gen LOGO)} & 0.789 & --- \\
44	\multicolumn{3}{l}{5-gen LOGO (excl.\ Qwen3-8B)} & 0.800 & --- \\
45	\bottomrule
46	\end{tabular}
47	\caption{Leave-one-generator-out macro-F1 with bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals ($n$\,=\,1000) for 3-class depth estimation using the 7-feature ensemble. Each row holds out one generator for testing and trains on the remaining five.}
48	\label{tab:logo-results}
49	\end{table}
50	
51	
52	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
53	\label{sec:results-differential}
54	
55	Features exhibit \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity measures drift monotonically with depth, while distributional shape measures remain near-stationary.
56	This pattern is consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the token-level NLL distribution---the text becomes progressively more predictable (mean shifts), while the functional form of the distribution is preserved (shape is invariant).
57	
58	\begin{figure}[t]
59	\centering
60	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
61	\caption{Differential depth response: Spearman correlations between individual features and generation depth. Central-tendency and diversity features (hapax ratio, TTR, entropy mean) show strong monotonic drift, while shape features (entropy std, tail decay) remain near-stationary.}
62	\label{fig:feature-drift}
63	\end{figure}
64	
65	Individual-level Spearman correlations with depth confirm the split (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}):
66	drifting features show strong monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\rho = -0.558$, TTR $\rho = -0.542$, entropy mean $\rho = -0.475$),
67	while shape features show weak or negligible correlations (entropy std $|\rho| = 0.076$, tail decay $|\rho| = 0.089$).
68	
69	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
70	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} reports ablation results.
71	The full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.789) significantly outperforms both surface-only features (TTR + hapax; F1\,=\,0.729) and reference-only features (entropy statistics + tail decay; F1\,=\,0.745; McNemar $p \approx 0$).
72	Permutation importance ranks hapax ratio highest (0.269), followed by entropy mean (0.164) and TTR (0.159).
73	Surface features capture vocabulary impoverishment; reference features capture distributional shift---neither subsumes the other.
74	
75	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
76	To confirm that differential depth response is not an artifact of the Pythia-410M reference model, we compute features using three cross-family references: Pythia-410M, OLMo-2-1B, and Qwen3-0.6B.
77	All three achieve LOGO F1 between 0.80 and 0.82, with consistent drift directions across features.
78	The pattern generalizes across reference model architectures.
79	
80	
81	\begin{table}[t]
82	\centering
83	\small
84	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
85	\toprule
86	\textbf{Feature set} & \textbf{LOGO F1} & \textbf{Top feature (importance)} \\
87	\midrule
88	Full (7 features)     & 0.789 & hapax\_ratio (0.269) \\
89	Surface only (2)      & 0.729 & hapax\_ratio (0.320) \\
90	Reference only (5)    & 0.745 & entropy\_mean (0.218) \\
91	\bottomrule
92	\end{tabular}
93	\caption{Feature ablation (6-gen LOGO). Full $>$ surface = reference confirms complementarity between surface diversity features and reference-model distributional features.}
94	\label{tab:ablation}
95	\end{table}
96	
97	Leave-one-feature-out analysis confirms that 6 of 7 features contribute positively, with entropy\_max showing the largest marginal impact despite modest split frequency (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
98	
99	
100	\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}
101	\label{sec:results-baselines}
102	
103	\begin{figure}[t]
104	\centering
105	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.png}
106	\caption{LOGO macro-F1 with 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals for each detection baseline and the 7-feature ensemble. Single-detector CIs span 18--26\,pp; the ensemble CI spans 1.4\,pp.}
107	\label{fig:baseline-ci}
108	\end{figure}
109	
110	We repurpose four existing detection scores as ordinal depth predictors by training the same ordinal classifier on each score as a single feature, using identical LOGO evaluation (Figure~\ref{fig:baseline-ci}).
111	
112	\begin{table}[t]
113	\centering
114	\small
115	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
116	\toprule
117	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{LOGO F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
118	\midrule
119	7-feature ensemble & \textbf{0.800} & [0.786, 0.800] \\
120	Avg-LogProb        & 0.765 & [0.616, 0.820] \\
121	LD-Score            & 0.721 & [0.588, 0.770] \\
122	FastDetectGPT       & 0.627 & [0.511, 0.762] \\
123	Binoculars          & 0.579 & [0.405, 0.664] \\
124	\bottomrule
125	\end{tabular}
126	\caption{Ordinal depth classification: 7-feature ensemble vs.\ single detection baselines (5-gen LOGO). The ensemble outperforms the best single detector (Avg-LogProb) by +3.5\,pp (paired bootstrap $p < 0.001$, $\Delta$ CI [3.0, 4.0]).}
127	\label{tab:baselines}
128	\end{table}
129	
130	The ensemble outperforms all baselines in mean F1: +3.5\,pp over Avg-LogProb ($p < 0.001$, $\Delta$ CI [3.0, 4.0]), +7.9\,pp over LD-Score, +17.3\,pp over FastDetectGPT, and +22.1\,pp over Binoculars (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}).
131	
132	The critical distinction is \emph{reliability}.
133	The ensemble CI spans just 1.4\,pp [0.786, 0.800], while single-detector CIs span 18--26\,pp (e.g., Avg-LogProb [0.616, 0.820]).
134	A single detector's CI is wider than \emph{all pairwise detector differences}, meaning no single detector is reliably best across generators.
135	Each detector captures partial depth signal---Avg-LogProb tracks mean perplexity shift, LD-Score captures character-level distributional change---but each fails on specific generator families.
136	The 7-feature ensemble aggregates complementary signals, producing stable performance regardless of the held-out generator.
137	
138	
139	\subsection{Downstream Utility: A Negative Finding}
140	\label{sec:results-downstream}
141	
142	Can depth-aware data curation improve downstream language model quality?
143	We fine-tune OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) and evaluate perplexity on a held-out test set (Table~\ref{tab:downstream}).
144	
145	\begin{table}[t]
146	\centering
147	\small
148	\begin{tabular}{lrc}
149	\toprule
150	\textbf{Variant} & \textbf{$n$} & \textbf{Test PPL} \\
151	\midrule
152	All depths            & 12{,}299 & 2.335 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
153	Size-matched (random) & 3{,}951  & 2.367 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
154	Depth 0--1 only       & 3{,}951  & 2.387 $\pm$ 0.001 \\
155	Binary-filtered       & 3{,}951  & 2.389 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
156	Depth 0 only          & 797      & 2.681 $\pm$ 0.001 \\
157	\bottomrule
158	\end{tabular}
159	\caption{Downstream fine-tuning perplexity (OLMo-2-0425-1B, mean $\pm$ std across 3 seeds). At equal sample count, random subsampling (2.367) outperforms depth-aware curation (depth 0--1: 2.387, binary-filtered: 2.389).}
160	\label{tab:downstream}
161	\end{table}
162	
163	The results are unambiguous: data quantity dominates depth composition.
164	Training on all 12{,}299 samples yields the lowest perplexity (2.335).
165	At equal sample count ($n$\,=\,3{,}951), random subsampling (2.367) \emph{outperforms} both depth-stratified selection (depth 0--1: 2.387) and binary detection filtering (2.389).
166	Cross-seed variance is below 0.002 across all variants, confirming the robustness of this null result.
167	
168	Restricting training to human-only text (depth 0, $n$\,=\,797) produces a sharp perplexity increase to 2.681, driven primarily by the 5$\times$ reduction in data volume rather than depth composition per se.
169	
170	We report this negative finding transparently: depth fingerprinting is an effective \emph{diagnostic} tool for characterizing text provenance, not a \emph{curative} tool for data curation.
171	Knowing the generation depth of a document does not, by itself, make it more or less useful for training.
172
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\subsection{AI-Generated Text Detection}
5	\label{sec:related-detection}
6	
7	Methods for distinguishing human-written from machine-generated text have evolved from statistical tests on token-level distributions \cite{gltr,grover,release_strategies} to zero-shot approaches exploiting reference-model likelihoods \cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,dna_gpt} and learned classifiers over linguistic features \cite{feat_aug_transformer,reliable_detection,ld_score}.
8	Recent work has pursued diverse detection signals: \citet{intrinsic_dimension} use intrinsic dimensionality estimates, \citet{diveye} propose surprisal diversity features directly comparable to our shape-based measures, and \citet{generator_barrier} characterize the distributional gap between human and model outputs.
9	\citet{jawahar_survey} provide a comprehensive survey.
10	A persistent limitation is that all these methods produce binary (real vs.\ synthetic) decisions.
11	Even when detection scores correlate with generation depth, they exhibit high cross-generator variance when repurposed as ordinal predictors (CI spanning 0.41--0.82 in macro-F1; \S\ref{sec:results-baselines}), because each score captures a single distributional property that generalizes inconsistently.
12	\citet{paraphrase_evades} and \citet{detection_possibilities} further demonstrate that detection robustness degrades under distributional shift.
13	Our reference-model entropy features are functionally NLL-based out-of-distribution scores \cite{ood_baseline}; the contribution is not a new detection method but the task formulation (ordinal depth estimation) and the empirical finding that combining drifting and stationary features yields consistent cross-generator performance.
14	
15	\subsection{Model Collapse and Iterative Generation}
16	\label{sec:related-collapse}
17	
18	A growing body of work studies quality degradation when models are trained on their own outputs.
19	\citet{self_consuming_mad} demonstrate that self-consuming generative models progressively lose distributional coverage, and \citet{curse_of_recursion} show that model collapse is inevitable in successive fine-tuning rounds without fresh human data, though \citet{model_collapse_avoidable} identify conditions under which careful data mixing can mitigate this effect.
20	\citet{strong_model_collapse} extend these results to larger-scale settings.
21	These studies focus on \emph{training-loop} degradation, where model weights are updated on recursive data.
22	Our setting is different: we study \emph{inference chains}, where a fixed model generates text conditioned on its own prior output without retraining.
23	
24	Two concurrent lines of work are most directly relevant.
25	\citet{broken_telephone} analyze paraphrasing and translation chains, showing progressive information loss analogous to the telephone game.
26	\citet{markovian_chains} provide a theoretical framework for convergence in iterative text transformations, demonstrating that semantic-preservation constraints induce attractor dynamics that bound distinguishable depth to approximately three classes.
27	Our work differs in two respects: we study \emph{non-paraphrastic continuation} chains, where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal beyond the attractor bound; and we focus on \emph{estimating} depth from text features rather than characterizing convergence behavior.
28	
29	\subsection{Vocabulary Diversity and Stylometry}
30	\label{sec:related-diversity}
31	
32	Declining lexical diversity in LLM-generated text is documented in both training \cite{curious_decline} and deployment \cite{writing_diversity} settings.
33	Our surface features---type-token ratio and hapax ratio---instantiate this observation for inference chains, where we show monotonic diversity decline across generation depths without any model retraining.
34	
35	These features are classical stylometric measures with a long history in authorship attribution \cite{stamatatos_survey,koppel_authorship}.
36	\citet{uchendu_attribution} extend stylometric and neural methods to multi-class attribution of text to specific generators.
37	We push beyond generator identity to the \emph{depth} dimension: text from the same generator at different depths exhibits systematically different feature profiles, and these profiles generalize across generators.
38	Sampling strategies also affect diversity; \citet{nucleus_sampling} show that top-$p$ sampling mitigates some degeneration, but our results demonstrate that diversity still declines monotonically across recursive application of the same sampling procedure.
39	
40	\subsection{Data Quality and Provenance}
41	\label{sec:related-provenance}
42	
43	Scalable data curation relies on quality signals to filter web-crawled corpora.
44	\citet{dsir} select pretraining data to match a target distribution, \citet{qurating} learn quality scores from LLM judgments, and \citet{ccnet} filter Common Crawl via perplexity-based heuristics.
45	Generation depth is a complementary provenance signal: it characterizes \emph{how many steps removed} text is from a human source, orthogonal to content quality per se.
46	
47	An alternative approach is proactive provenance through watermarking \cite{watermark_llm,synthid_text,aaronson_watermark}, where identifiable patterns are embedded during generation.
48	However, \citet{chainwash} demonstrate that watermark strength decays across multi-step generation chains, motivating passive post-hoc methods that do not require generator cooperation.
49	Our depth fingerprinting is such a method: it operates on text alone without assuming access to the generating model or an embedded watermark.
50	At the feature level, our reference-model NLL analysis connects to \citet{min_k_percent}, who use the probability distribution's lower tail for membership inference; we analyze the full NLL distribution for a different purpose---ordinal depth estimation rather than training-set membership.
51	
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Task Formulation}
5	\label{sec:task}
6	
7	Given a text $x$, the goal is to predict its \emph{generation depth} $d \in \{0, 1, 2{+}\}$, where $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text, $d{=}1$ denotes first-generation LLM output conditioned on human text, and $d{=}2{+}$ denotes text produced by recursively conditioning an LLM on its own prior output at least twice.
8	Formally, let $\mathcal{C}_0$ be a reference corpus of human-written text.
9	A generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \sim \mathcal{C}_0$, and $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic LLM generation conditioned on its input.
10	We frame depth estimation as an ordinal classification task over three classes rather than binary real-vs-synthetic detection, enabling finer-grained provenance characterization.
11	Depths beyond 2 are merged into a single $2{+}$ class, as preliminary experiments showed diminishing discriminability at higher depths---consistent with the monotonic convergence of distributional features reported in \S\ref{sec:results}.
12	
13	\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}
14	\label{sec:generation}
15	
16	We construct generation chains using six generators spanning three model families: \textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \cite{pythia}, \textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \cite{olmo}, and \textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \cite{qwen3}.
17	This selection covers parameter scales from 0.6B to 8.2B and diverse pretraining corpora, enabling evaluation of cross-generator and cross-scale generalization.
18	
19	For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\mathcal{C}_0$.
20	Each seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\texttt{Continue the following text: \{text\}}''.
21	All generation uses temperature $\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.
22	The resulting dataset contains approximately 27{,}000 texts across all generators and depths.
23	For classification, depths 2--4 are collapsed into the $2{+}$ class.
24	
25	\subsection{Feature Extraction}
26	\label{sec:features}
27	
28	We extract seven distributional features per text, organized into two groups based on what they measure rather than how they are computed.
29	
30	\paragraph{Reference-model features.}
31	A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than and architecturally independent of all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.
32	From the resulting NLL sequence, we extract four statistics:
33	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
34	    \item \textbf{entropy\_mean}: mean token-level NLL, capturing the average predictability of the text under the reference model;
35	    \item \textbf{entropy\_std}: standard deviation of token-level NLL, measuring the spread of per-token surprise;
36	    \item \textbf{entropy\_max}: maximum token-level NLL, capturing the most surprising token;
37	    \item \textbf{tail\_decay}: proportion of tokens whose NLL exceeds the 90th percentile of the reference distribution (computed over $\mathcal{C}_0$), measuring how heavy-tailed the NLL distribution is relative to human text.
38	\end{itemize}
39	
40	\paragraph{Surface features.}
41	Three features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model:
42	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
43	    \item \textbf{TTR}: type-token ratio (number of unique word types divided by total tokens);
44	    \item \textbf{hapax\_ratio}: proportion of words appearing exactly once (\emph{hapax legomena});
45	    \item \textbf{entropy\_min}: minimum token-level NLL under the reference model---entropy\_min is computed via the reference model but captures token-level predictability (extremely predictable tokens); in ablation experiments (\S\ref{sec:results}), it is grouped with reference features.
46	\end{itemize}
47	
48	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
49	The grouping reflects an empirical pattern we term \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features (entropy\_mean, TTR, hapax\_ratio) drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy\_std, tail\_decay) remain near-stationary.
50	This differential behavior---consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the NLL distribution while preserving its functional form---motivates combining both feature types.
51	As shown in \S\ref{sec:results}, the full 7-feature set significantly outperforms either group alone.
52	
53	\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}
54	\label{sec:evaluation}
55	
56	\paragraph{Cross-validation.}
57	To evaluate generalization to unseen generators, we use \textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: in each fold, one of the six generators is held out for testing and the remaining five provide training data.
58	We additionally report \textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} results, where an entire model family (e.g., all Qwen models) is held out, testing generalization across architectural lineages.
59	
60	\paragraph{Classifier.}
61	We use XGBoost \cite{xgboost} as the classifier throughout.
62	Gradient-boosted trees handle heterogeneous feature scales and nonlinear interactions without extensive hyperparameter tuning, and their feature importance scores support interpretability.
63	The same hyperparameter configuration is used across all experiments to avoid fold-specific overfitting.
64	LOGO F1 is stable across random seeds (mean\,=\,0.789, std\,=\,0.0009 over 5 seeds), confirming that results are not sensitive to classifier initialization.
65	Alternative classifiers (random forest, logistic regression) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}), confirming that discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.
66	
67	\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}
68	The primary metric is \textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, which weights minority classes (gen-1, gen-2+) equally against the typically easier gen-0 class.
69	We report per-class F1 scores and 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals ($n{=}1{,}000$ resamples).
70	For pairwise model comparisons (e.g., full features vs.\ ablated subsets), we use the \textbf{McNemar test} on disagreement tables.
71	For comparisons against detection baselines, we use \textbf{paired bootstrap} significance tests.
72	All reported $p$-values are two-sided.
73	
74	\paragraph{Baselines.}
75	We compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as ordinal depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \cite{ld_score}.
76	Each baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation, ensuring a controlled comparison.
77	
78	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
79	Since TTR and hapax\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify that depth signal is not an artifact of systematic length variation.
80	We conduct three complementary analyses: (1) Spearman correlation between text length and each feature (requiring $|\rho| < 0.15$ for non-confounded features), (2) classification with length-normalized features, and (3) comparison on length-matched subsets.
81	
```

```text
1	% EMNLP 2026 ARR Submission — ACL 2023 Template

13	\title{Depth Fingerprinting: Ordinal Generation Depth Estimation\\in Non-Paraphrastic LLM Text Chains}
14	
15	\author{Anonymous}
16	
17	\begin{document}
18	\maketitle
19	
20	\begin{abstract}
21	As large language model (LLM) outputs accumulate on the web, training corpora increasingly contain recursively generated text of unknown provenance depth.
22	We formulate \emph{ordinal depth estimation}---classifying whether text is human-written (gen-0), first-generation synthetic (gen-1), or deeper (gen-2+)---a task that moves beyond binary real-versus-synthetic detection.
23	Existing detectors provide only binary scores and, when repurposed for ordinal prediction, exhibit high cross-generator variance (confidence intervals spanning 0.41--0.82), making them unreliable depth estimators.
24	We characterize a \emph{differential depth response} in distributional features computed under a held-out reference model: central-tendency and diversity features (entropy mean, type-token ratio, hapax ratio) drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay rate) remain near-stationary.
25	Combining both feature types into a 7-feature ensemble exploits this complementarity: the full set (macro-F1\,=\,0.79) outperforms surface-only (0.73) and reference-only (0.75) subsets.
26	In leave-one-generator-out evaluation across 6 generators from 3 model families (0.6B--8.2B parameters), the ensemble achieves macro-F1 of 0.79 ($p < 0.001$ vs.\ all single-detector baselines) with tight cross-generator consistency (CI width 1.4\,pp vs.\ 18--26\,pp for single detectors).
27	However, depth-aware data curation does not outperform random subsampling---data quantity dominates depth composition---positioning depth fingerprinting as a diagnostic tool for provenance characterization, not a curative tool for data selection.
28	\end{abstract}
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced ordinal generation depth estimation as a task beyond binary synthetic text detection, providing a framework for characterizing the provenance of web-crawled text at finer granularity.
5	Across six generators from three model families (0.6B--8.2B parameters), we documented differential depth response: central-tendency and diversity features drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features remain near-stationary.
6	A 7-feature ensemble combining both feature types achieves leave-one-generator-out macro-F1 of 0.79, with per-generator held-out F1 ranging from 0.68 to 0.86.
7	We also reported that depth-aware data curation does not improve downstream language model quality over random subsampling, establishing depth fingerprinting as a diagnostic rather than curative tool.
8	
9	Future work should extend evaluation to frontier-scale generators (70B+), mixed-generator chains that better approximate real-world contamination, and multilingual settings.
10	
```

```json
      "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe introduced ordinal generation depth estimation as a task beyond binary synthetic text detection, providing a framework for characterizing the provenance of web-crawled text at finer granularity.\nAcross six generators from three model families (0.6B--8.2B parameters), we documented differential depth response: central-tendency and diversity features drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features remain near-stationary.\nA 7-feature ensemble combining both feature types achieves leave-one-generator-out macro-F1 of 0.79, with per-generator held-out F1 ranging from 0.68 to 0.86.\nWe also reported that depth-aware data curation does not improve downstream language model quality over random subsampling, establishing depth fingerprinting as a diagnostic rather than curative tool.\n\nFuture work should extend evaluation to frontier-scale generators (70B+), mixed-generator chains that better approximate real-world contamination, and multilingual settings.\n",
      "numLines": 10,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 10
    }
  },
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WRYbXTLdtPj7QjdLTJ6goj`</summary>

```text
1	% Abstract — Depth Fingerprinting of Recursive LLM Generation Chains
2	\begin{abstract}
3	As large language model (LLM) outputs accumulate on the web, training corpora increasingly contain recursively generated text of unknown provenance depth.
4	We formulate \emph{ordinal depth estimation}---classifying whether text is human-written (gen-0), first-generation synthetic (gen-1), or deeper (gen-2+)---a task that moves beyond binary real-versus-synthetic detection.
5	Existing detectors provide only binary scores and, when repurposed for ordinal prediction, exhibit high cross-generator variance (confidence intervals spanning 0.41--0.82), making them unreliable depth estimators.
6	We characterize a \emph{differential depth response} in distributional features computed under a held-out reference model: central-tendency and diversity features (entropy mean, type-token ratio, hapax ratio) drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay rate) remain near-stationary.
7	Combining both feature types into a 7-feature ensemble exploits this complementarity: the full set (macro-F1\,=\,0.79) outperforms surface-only (0.73) and reference-only (0.75) subsets.
8	In leave-one-generator-out evaluation across 6 generators from 3 model families (0.6B--8.2B parameters), the ensemble achieves macro-F1 of 0.79 ($p < 0.001$ vs.\ all single-detector baselines) with tight cross-generator consistency (CI width 1.4\,pp vs.\ 18--26\,pp for single detectors).
9	However, depth-aware data curation does not outperform random subsampling---data quantity dominates depth composition---positioning depth fingerprinting as a diagnostic tool for provenance characterization, not a curative tool for data selection.
10	\end{abstract}
11	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Web-crawled corpora are the dominant source of pretraining data for large language models (LLMs), yet an increasing fraction of this data is itself LLM-generated.
5	Estimates suggest that 7--17\% of recent scientific papers and peer reviews contain substantial LLM-modified content \cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}, and this proportion continues to grow as generated text enters the web and becomes seed material for subsequent generation.
6	The result is recursive generation chains whose outputs progressively diverge from human-written text---a problem that current detection methods are ill-equipped to address.
7	
8	Existing detectors \cite{fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score,detectgpt} provide binary real-vs-synthetic classification, forcing an all-or-nothing filtering decision: discard everything flagged as synthetic, losing potentially useful first-generation content, or retain everything, admitting text with compounding distributional drift.
9	This conflates qualitatively different material.
10	A first-generation continuation---an LLM expanding a human-written passage---retains much of the source distribution's lexical and structural properties.
11	A third- or fourth-generation text, conditioned recursively on prior LLM outputs, exhibits measurably reduced vocabulary diversity, compressed perplexity, and narrower token probability distributions.
12	Treating these as a single ``synthetic'' category discards provenance information that could inform risk-stratified curation decisions.
13	
14	We argue that a finer-grained characterization is needed: \emph{ordinal depth estimation}, the task of classifying text into generation depth classes (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+) rather than producing a binary label.
15	No existing method addresses this task directly.
16	When repurposed as ordinal depth predictors, individual detection scores exhibit high cross-generator variance---confidence intervals spanning 0.41--0.82 in macro-F1 (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines})---making any single detector unreliable across unknown generators.
17	Concurrent work on iterative paraphrasing chains \cite{broken_telephone,markovian_chains} analyzes convergence behavior under semantic-preservation constraints, which bound distinguishable depth classes to approximately three.
18	These results do not extend to \emph{non-paraphrastic} generation chains (e.g., continuation), where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal.
19	
20	We study distributional drift in non-paraphrastic LLM generation chains across six generators from three model families (Pythia, OLMo, Qwen; 0.6B--8.2B parameters).
21	Our central empirical finding is \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features---including reference-model mean entropy (functionally an NLL-based out-of-distribution score \cite{ood_baseline}), type-token ratio, and hapax ratio---drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary.
22	This pattern is consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the NLL distribution: text becomes progressively more predictable on average while the functional form of the distribution is preserved.
23	The combination of drifting and stationary features yields a 7-feature ensemble that achieves leave-one-generator-out (LOGO) macro-F1 of 0.79, with per-generator held-out F1 ranging from 0.68 to 0.86.
24	This ensemble maintains tight cross-generator consistency (CI width 1.4\,pp), whereas the best single detector spans over 20\,pp.
25	The practical advantage is reliability across unknown generators, not just average performance.
26	
27	We also report a negative finding.
28	Depth-aware data curation---selecting training data based on estimated generation depth---does not improve downstream language model quality compared to random subsampling at equal sample count (perplexity: 2.367 random vs.\ 2.387 depth-stratified).
29	Depth fingerprinting is an effective \emph{diagnostic} tool for characterizing text provenance, not a \emph{curative} tool for data selection.
30	
31	Our contributions are:
32	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
33	    \item \textbf{Task formulation.} We define ordinal generation depth estimation as a task beyond binary detection, providing a framework for finer-grained provenance characterization of web-crawled text.
34	    \item \textbf{Empirical characterization.} We document differential depth response across six generators spanning three model families (0.6B--8.2B): a 7-feature ensemble combining drifting and stationary features achieves LOGO F1\,=\,0.79, significantly outperforming all single-detector baselines ($p < 0.001$) with tight cross-generator consistency.
35	    \item \textbf{Negative finding.} Depth-aware data curation does not outperform random subsampling for downstream CLM quality (size-matched 2.367 vs.\ depth-stratified 2.387), establishing depth fingerprinting as diagnostic rather than curative.
36	\end{enumerate}
37	
38	The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.
39	\S\ref{sec:related} surveys related work on text detection, model collapse, and data quality.
40	\S\ref{sec:method} describes generation chain construction, feature extraction, and evaluation protocol.
41	\S\ref{sec:results} presents classification results, differential depth response analysis, baseline comparisons, and the downstream utility experiment.
42	\S\ref{sec:discussion} discusses limitations and future directions.
43	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Qi1d9zdxmzHrXLqvJNgVCX`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Why are shape features stationary?}
5	Distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary across generation depths, while central-tendency features drift monotonically (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
6	One \emph{speculative} explanation is that shape is primarily determined by the sampling mechanism (temperature, top-$p$ truncation) and the model's architecture-level output distribution, both of which are fixed across generation steps.
7	Recursive generation shifts \emph{what} the model talks about---steering toward higher-probability vocabulary regions---but does not alter \emph{how} the model distributes probability mass around its predictions.
8	If this account is correct, shape features should remain stationary even at much greater depths and across different prompt types, while level features should continue to drift until reaching a fixed point.
9	We flag this as a hypothesis for future investigation, not a conclusion supported by our current experiments.
10	
11	\paragraph{Relation to OOD detection.}
12	Reference-model entropy features are functionally NLL-based out-of-distribution scores---the same signal backbone underlying DetectGPT \cite{detectgpt}, GLTR \cite{gltr}, and Binoculars \cite{binoculars}.
13	Our contribution is not these features per se, but the \emph{ordinal} depth formulation and the empirical finding that they exhibit differential depth response under recursive generation.
14	This connection also offers a lens on shape stationarity: in the OOD detection literature, distributional location statistics (mean log-likelihood) shift under domain drift, while shape statistics (variance, tail behavior) are more robust \cite{ood_baseline}.
15	Recursive generation chains produce an analogous effect---progressive drift away from human text that manifests primarily as a location shift in the NLL distribution.
16	
17	\paragraph{Practical implications.}
18	The differential depth response enables risk-stratified data curation.
19	Rather than binary keep-or-discard filtering, practitioners can flag gen-2+ text for manual review while retaining gen-1 content that preserves much of the source distribution's properties.
20	Three-class granularity is well-suited to this use case: it separates human text from moderate-drift first-generation content and from deeper text with compounding distributional shift---the distinction most relevant to curation decisions.
21	Finer-grained depth distinctions may be achievable for non-paraphrastic chains (unlike paraphrastic chains, which converge within $\sim$3 steps \cite{broken_telephone}), but the three-class scheme already captures the practically relevant boundaries.
22	The tight cross-generator consistency of the ensemble (CI width 1.4\,pp) makes deployment feasible even when the generating model is unknown.
23	
24	\paragraph{Information value of the negative finding.}
25	Depth-aware curation does not improve downstream language model quality over random subsampling at equal sample count (\S\ref{sec:results-downstream}).
26	This negative result carries positive information value: it rules out depth-based filtering as a data selection strategy, sparing practitioners from building depth-conditional pipelines that would not yield quality gains.
27	The dominant factor is data quantity, not depth composition---consistent with findings emphasizing data volume over curation sophistication \cite{self_consuming_mad}.
28	
29	\paragraph{Connection to model collapse.}
30	Our results relate to, but are distinct from, model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse,self_consuming_mad}.
31	Model collapse describes distributional degradation when models are \emph{retrained} on their own outputs across generations.
32	We observe analogous drift---monotonic decrease in vocabulary diversity, compression of perplexity---in \emph{inference chains} without any parameter updates.
33	This suggests that the mechanisms documented in model collapse theory (regression toward high-probability modes, loss of distributional tails) operate at inference time as well, driven by the sampling process rather than gradient updates.
34	The shared phenomenology motivates using depth fingerprinting features as early indicators of data that, if fed back into training, may accelerate model collapse.
35	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01XP7yU7RFY1bdSoPxf6sCUg`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	Our experiments include generators up to 8.2B parameters (Qwen3-8B).
5	Depth signal persists at this scale, but cross-scale classifier transfer incurs a moderate gap (held-out F1\,=\,0.683 vs.\ 0.76--0.86 for $\leq$2.8B generators).
6	Frontier models (70B+) remain untested; their higher fluency may further compress feature margins.
7	
8	We evaluate three prompt types (continuation, elaboration, rewriting; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}) but all share the same Pythia-2.8B generator.
9	Cross-prompt-type transfer (LOPTO F1\,=\,0.753) confirms the depth signal generalizes, yet additional prompt types (e.g., summarization, instruction following) and cross-generator $\times$ cross-prompt interactions remain untested.
10	
11	Chains are constructed with a single generator model per step.
12	Real-world web contamination likely involves heterogeneous generators at each depth, which could attenuate or alter the depth signal we observe.
13	We also do not evaluate robustness to human editing or post-processing of generated text, which is common in practice and may disrupt distributional features.
14	
15	The negative downstream result (\S\ref{sec:results-downstream}) is evaluated on causal language modeling only.
16	Other downstream tasks (classification, summarization, instruction following) may respond differently to depth composition.
17	
18	We use 3-class granularity (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+). A 5-class evaluation (Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}) yields LOGO F1\,$\approx$\,0.50, with depths 2--4 mutually indistinguishable, validating the 3-class design as the practical resolution limit.
19	
```

```json
      "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nOur experiments include generators up to 8.2B parameters (Qwen3-8B).\nDepth signal persists at this scale, but cross-scale classifier transfer incurs a moderate gap (held-out F1\\,=\\,0.683 vs.\\ 0.76--0.86 for $\\leq$2.8B generators).\nFrontier models (70B+) remain untested; their higher fluency may further compress feature margins.\n\nWe evaluate three prompt types (continuation, elaboration, rewriting; Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}) but all share the same Pythia-2.8B generator.\nCross-prompt-type transfer (LOPTO F1\\,=\\,0.753) confirms the depth signal generalizes, yet additional prompt types (e.g., summarization, instruction following) and cross-generator $\\times$ cross-prompt interactions remain untested.\n\nChains are constructed with a single generator model per step.\nReal-world web contamination likely involves heterogeneous generators at each depth, which could attenuate or alter the depth signal we observe.\nWe also do not evaluate robustness to human editing or post-processing of generated text, which is common in practice and may disrupt distributional features.\n\nThe negative downstream result (\\S\\ref{sec:results-downstream}) is evaluated on causal language modeling only.\nOther downstream tasks (classification, summarization, instruction following) may respond differently to depth composition.\n\nWe use 3-class granularity (gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+). A 5-class evaluation (Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}) yields LOGO F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.50, with depths 2--4 mutually indistinguishable, validating the 3-class design as the practical resolution limit.\n",
      "numLines": 19,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 19
    }
  },
```

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "Our reference-model entropy features are functionally NLL-based out-of-distribution scores \\cite{ood_baseline}; the contribution is not a new detection method but the task formulation (ordinal depth estimation) and the empirical finding that combining drifting and stationary features yields consistent cross-generator performance.\n\n\\subsection{Model Collapse and Iterative Generation}",
  "new_string": "Our reference-model entropy features are functionally NLL-based out-of-distribution scores \\cite{ood_baseline}; the contribution is not a new detection method but the task formulation (ordinal depth estimation) and the empirical finding that combining drifting and stationary features yields consistent cross-generator performance.\n\n\\paragraph{Source attribution vs.\\ depth estimation.}\nA related but distinct task is \\emph{source attribution}: identifying \\emph{which} model generated a given text.\n\\citet{uchendu_attribution} train multi-class classifiers over stylometric features to distinguish outputs of different generators, and \\citet{munir_looking_glass} learn attribution models that generalize across unseen language models.\nThese methods answer \\emph{who} generated the text; depth estimation answers \\emph{how many recursive steps} produced it.\nThe two tasks are orthogonal: a single generator can produce text at any depth, and texts at the same depth may originate from different generators.\nOur LOGO evaluation explicitly decouples generator identity from depth by holding out entire generators, ensuring that the classifier learns depth signal rather than generator fingerprints.\n\n\\subsection{Model Collapse and Iterative Generation}"
}
```

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "b1b4ff69-ada4-4097-a802-609dcf42084f",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "93d04b10-c0e1-4a9a-8980-83118df7611f",

  "uuid": "208d6445-e7fc-43f9-8a33-4d4157069b16",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "Our reference-model entropy features are functionally NLL-based out-of-distribution scores \\cite{ood_baseline}; the contribution is not a new detection method but the task formulation (ordinal depth estimation) and the empirical finding that combining drifting and stationary features yields consistent cross-generator performance.\n\n\\subsection{Model Collapse and Iterative Generation}",
    "newString": "Our reference-model entropy features are functionally NLL-based out-of-distribution scores \\cite{ood_baseline}; the contribution is not a new detection method but the task formulation (ordinal depth estimation) and the empirical finding that combining drifting and stationary features yields consistent cross-generator performance.\n\n\\paragraph{Source attribution vs.\\ depth estimation.}\nA related but distinct task is \\emph{source attribution}: identifying \\emph{which} model generated a given text.\n\\citet{uchendu_attribution} train multi-class classifiers over stylometric features to distinguish outputs of different generators, and \\citet{munir_looking_glass} learn attribution models that generalize across unseen language models.\nThese methods answer \\emph{who} generated the text; depth estimation answers \\emph{how many recursive steps} produced it.\nThe two tasks are orthogonal: a single generator can produce text at any depth, and texts at the same depth may originate from different generators.\nOur LOGO evaluation explicitly decouples generator identity from depth by holding out entire generators, ensuring that the classifier learns depth signal rather than generator fingerprints.\n\n\\subsection{Model Collapse and Iterative Generation}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\subsection{AI-Generated Text Detection}\n\\label{sec:related-detection}\n\nMethods for distinguishing human-written from machine-generated text have evolved from statistical tests on token-level distributions \\cite{gltr,grover,release_strategies} to zero-shot approaches exploiting reference-model likelihoods \\cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,dna_gpt} and learned classifiers over linguistic features \\cite{feat_aug_transformer,reliable_detection,ld_score}.\nRecent work has pursued diverse detection signals: \\citet{intrinsic_dimension} use intrinsic dimensionality estimates, \\citet{diveye} propose surprisal diversity features directly comparable to our shape-based measures, and \\citet{generator_barrier} characterize the distributional gap between human and model outputs.\n\\citet{jawahar_survey} provide a comprehensive survey.\nA persistent limitation is that all these methods produce binary (real vs.\\ synthetic) decisions.\nEven when detection scores correlate with generation depth, they exhibit high cross-generator variance when repurposed as ordinal predictors (CI spanning 0.41--0.82 in macro-F1; \\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}), because each score captures a single distributional property that generalizes inconsistently.\n\\citet{paraphrase_evades} and \\citet{detection_possibilities} further demonstrate that detection robustness degrades under distributional shift.\nOur reference-model entropy features are functionally NLL-based out-of-distribution scores \\cite{ood_baseline}; the contribution is not a new detection method but the task formulation (ordinal depth estimation) and the empirical finding that combining drifting and stationary features yields consistent cross-generator performance.\n\n\\subsection{Model Collapse and Iterative Generation}\n\\label{sec:related-collapse}\n\nA growing body of work studies quality degradation when models are trained on their own outputs.\n\\citet{self_consuming_mad} demonstrate that self-consuming generative models progressively lose distributional coverage, and \\citet{curse_of_recursion} show that model collapse is inevitable in successive fine-tuning rounds without fresh human data, though \\citet{model_collapse_avoidable} identify conditions under which careful data mixing can mitigate this effect.\n\\citet{strong_model_collapse} extend these results to larger-scale settings.\nThese studies focus on \\emph{training-loop} degradation, where model weights are updated on recursive data.\nOur setting is different: we study \\emph{inference chains}, where a fixed model generates text conditioned on its own prior output without retraining.\n\nTwo concurrent lines of work are most directly relevant.\n\\citet{broken_telephone} analyze paraphrasing and translation chains, showing progressive information loss analogous to the telephone game.\n\\citet{markovian_chains} provide a theoretical framework for convergence in iterative text transformations, demonstrating that semantic-preservation constraints induce attractor dynamics that bound distinguishable depth to approximately three classes.\nOur work differs in two respects: we study \\emph{non-paraphrastic continuation} chains, where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal beyond the attractor bound; and we focus on \\emph{estimating} depth from text features rather than characterizing convergence behavior.\n\n\\subsection{Vocabulary Diversity and Stylometry}\n\\label{sec:related-diversity}\n\nDeclining lexical diversity in LLM-generated text is documented in both training \\cite{curious_decline} and deployment \\cite{writing_diversity} settings.\nOur surface features---type-token ratio and hapax ratio---instantiate this observation for inference chains, where we show monotonic diversity decline across generation depths without any model retraining.\n\nThese features are classical stylometric measures with a long history in authorship attribution \\cite{stamatatos_survey,koppel_authorship}.\n\\citet{uchendu_attribution} extend stylometric and neural methods to multi-class attribution of text to specific generators.\nWe push beyond generator identity to the \\emph{depth} dimension: text from the same generator at different depths exhibits systematically different feature profiles, and these profiles generalize across generators.\nSampling strategies also affect diversity; \\citet{nucleus_sampling} show that top-$p$ sampling mitigates some degeneration, but our results demonstrate that diversity still declines monotonically across recursive application of the same sampling procedure.\n\n\\subsection{Data Quality and Provenance}\n\\label{sec:related-provenance}\n\nScalable data curation relies on quality signals to filter web-crawled corpora.\n\\citet{dsir} select pretraining data to match a target distribution, \\citet{qurating} learn quality scores from LLM judgments, and \\citet{ccnet} filter Common Crawl via perplexity-based heuristics.\nGeneration depth is a complementary provenance signal: it characterizes \\emph{how many steps removed} text is from a human source, orthogonal to content quality per se.\n\nAn alternative approach is proactive provenance through watermarking \\cite{watermark_llm,synthid_text,aaronson_watermark}, where identifiable patterns are embedded during generation.\nHowever, \\citet{chainwash} demonstrate that watermark strength decays across multi-step generation chains, motivating passive post-hoc methods that do not require generator cooperation.\nOur depth fingerprinting is such a method: it operates on text alone without assuming access to the generating model or an embedded watermark.\nAt the feature level, our reference-model NLL analysis connects to \\citet{min_k_percent}, who use the probability distribution's lower tail for membership inference; we analyze the full NLL distribution for a different purpose---ordinal depth estimation rather than training-set membership.\n",
```

```json
    "oldString": "    \\item \\textbf{Negative finding.} Depth-aware data curation does not outperform random subsampling for downstream CLM quality (size-matched 2.367 vs.\\ depth-stratified 2.387), establishing depth fingerprinting as diagnostic rather than curative.",
    "newString": "    \\item \\textbf{Complementary analysis.} As a complementary analysis, we show that depth-aware data curation does not improve downstream language model quality compared to random subsampling, establishing depth fingerprinting as a diagnostic rather than curative tool.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWeb-crawled corpora are the dominant source of pretraining data for large language models (LLMs), yet an increasing fraction of this data is itself LLM-generated.\nEstimates suggest that 7--17\\% of recent scientific papers and peer reviews contain substantial LLM-modified content \\cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}, and this proportion continues to grow as generated text enters the web and becomes seed material for subsequent generation.\nThe result is recursive generation chains whose outputs progressively diverge from human-written text---a problem that current detection methods are ill-equipped to address.\n\nExisting detectors \\cite{fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score,detectgpt} provide binary real-vs-synthetic classification, forcing an all-or-nothing filtering decision: discard everything flagged as synthetic, losing potentially useful first-generation content, or retain everything, admitting text with compounding distributional drift.\nThis conflates qualitatively different material.\nA first-generation continuation---an LLM expanding a human-written passage---retains much of the source distribution's lexical and structural properties.\nA third- or fourth-generation text, conditioned recursively on prior LLM outputs, exhibits measurably reduced vocabulary diversity, compressed perplexity, and narrower token probability distributions.\nTreating these as a single ``synthetic'' category discards provenance information that could inform risk-stratified curation decisions.\n\nWe argue that a finer-grained characterization is needed: \\emph{ordinal depth estimation}, the task of classifying text into generation depth classes (gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+) rather than producing a binary label.\nNo existing method addresses this task directly.\nWhen repurposed as ordinal depth predictors, individual detection scores exhibit high cross-generator variance---confidence intervals spanning 0.41--0.82 in macro-F1 (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines})---making any single detector unreliable across unknown generators.\nConcurrent work on iterative paraphrasing chains \\cite{broken_telephone,markovian_chains} analyzes convergence behavior under semantic-preservation constraints, which bound distinguishable depth classes to approximately three.\nThese results do not extend to \\emph{non-paraphrastic} generation chains (e.g., continuation), where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal.\n\nWe study distributional drift in non-paraphrastic LLM generation chains across six generators from three model families (Pythia, OLMo, Qwen; 0.6B--8.2B parameters).\nOur central empirical finding is \\emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features---including reference-model mean entropy (functionally an NLL-based out-of-distribution score \\cite{ood_baseline}), type-token ratio, and hapax ratio---drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary.\nThis pattern is consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the NLL distribution: text becomes progressively more predictable on average while the functional form of the distribution is preserved.\nThe combination of drifting and stationary features yields a 7-feature ensemble that achieves leave-one-generator-out (LOGO) macro-F1 of 0.79, with per-generator held-out F1 ranging from 0.68 to 0.86.\nThis ensemble maintains tight cross-generator consistency (CI width 1.4\\,pp), whereas the best single detector spans over 20\\,pp.\nThe practical advantage is reliability across unknown generators, not just average performance.\n\nWe also report a negative finding.\nDepth-aware data curation---selecting training data based on estimated generation depth---does not improve downstream language model quality compared to random subsampling at equal sample count (perplexity: 2.367 random vs.\\ 2.387 depth-stratified).\nDepth fingerprinting is an effective \\emph{diagnostic} tool for characterizing text provenance, not a \\emph{curative} tool for data selection.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formulation.} We define ordinal generation depth estimation as a task beyond binary detection, providing a framework for finer-grained provenance characterization of web-crawled text.\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical characterization.} We document differential depth response across six generators spanning three model families (0.6B--8.2B): a 7-feature ensemble combining drifting and stationary features achieves LOGO F1\\,=\\,0.79, significantly outperforming all single-detector baselines ($p < 0.001$) with tight cross-generator consistency.\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative finding.} Depth-aware data curation does not outperform random subsampling for downstream CLM quality (size-matched 2.367 vs.\\ depth-stratified 2.387), establishing depth fingerprinting as diagnostic rather than curative.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nThe remainder of this paper is organized as follows.\n\\S\\ref{sec:related} surveys related work on text detection, model collapse, and data quality.\n\\S\\ref{sec:method} describes generation chain construction, feature extraction, and evaluation protocol.\n\\S\\ref{sec:results} presents classification results, differential depth response analysis, baseline comparisons, and the downstream utility experiment.\n\\S\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses limitations and future directions.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

          "     \\item \\textbf{Empirical characterization.} We document differential depth response across six generators spanning three model families (0.6B--8.2B): a 7-feature ensemble combining drifting and stationary features achieves LOGO F1\\,=\\,0.79, significantly outperforming all single-detector baselines ($p < 0.001$) with tight cross-generator consistency.",
          "-    \\item \\textbf{Negative finding.} Depth-aware data curation does not outperform random subsampling for downstream CLM quality (size-matched 2.367 vs.\\ depth-stratified 2.387), establishing depth fingerprinting as diagnostic rather than curative.",
```

```json
    "oldString": "However, depth-aware data curation does not outperform random subsampling---data quantity dominates depth composition---positioning depth fingerprinting as a diagnostic tool for provenance characterization, not a curative tool for data selection.",
    "newString": "A complementary analysis shows that depth-aware data curation does not outperform random subsampling---data quantity dominates depth composition---positioning depth fingerprinting as a diagnostic tool for provenance characterization, not a curative tool for data selection.",
    "originalFile": "% Abstract — Depth Fingerprinting of Recursive LLM Generation Chains\n\\begin{abstract}\nAs large language model (LLM) outputs accumulate on the web, training corpora increasingly contain recursively generated text of unknown provenance depth.\nWe formulate \\emph{ordinal depth estimation}---classifying whether text is human-written (gen-0), first-generation synthetic (gen-1), or deeper (gen-2+)---a task that moves beyond binary real-versus-synthetic detection.\nExisting detectors provide only binary scores and, when repurposed for ordinal prediction, exhibit high cross-generator variance (confidence intervals spanning 0.41--0.82), making them unreliable depth estimators.\nWe characterize a \\emph{differential depth response} in distributional features computed under a held-out reference model: central-tendency and diversity features (entropy mean, type-token ratio, hapax ratio) drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay rate) remain near-stationary.\nCombining both feature types into a 7-feature ensemble exploits this complementarity: the full set (macro-F1\\,=\\,0.79) outperforms surface-only (0.73) and reference-only (0.75) subsets.\nIn leave-one-generator-out evaluation across 6 generators from 3 model families (0.6B--8.2B parameters), the ensemble achieves macro-F1 of 0.79 ($p < 0.001$ vs.\\ all single-detector baselines) with tight cross-generator consistency (CI width 1.4\\,pp vs.\\ 18--26\\,pp for single detectors).\nHowever, depth-aware data curation does not outperform random subsampling---data quantity dominates depth composition---positioning depth fingerprinting as a diagnostic tool for provenance characterization, not a curative tool for data selection.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

        "oldLines": 5,
        "newStart": 6,
```

```json
{
```

```json
  "old_string": "A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than and architecturally independent of all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.\nFrom the resulting NLL sequence, we extract four statistics:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_mean}: mean token-level NLL, capturing the average predictability of the text under the reference model;\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_std}: standard deviation of token-level NLL, measuring the spread of per-token surprise;\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_max}: maximum token-level NLL, capturing the most surprising token;\n    \\item \\textbf{tail\\_decay}: proportion of tokens whose NLL exceeds the 90th percentile of the reference distribution (computed over $\\mathcal{C}_0$), measuring how heavy-tailed the NLL distribution is relative to human text.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Surface features.}\nThree features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{TTR}: type-token ratio (number of unique word types divided by total tokens);\n    \\item \\textbf{hapax\\_ratio}: proportion of words appearing exactly once (\\emph{hapax legomena});\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_min}: minimum token-level NLL under the reference model---entropy\\_min is computed via the reference model but captures token-level predictability (extremely predictable tokens); in ablation experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:results}), it is grouped with reference features.\n\\end{itemize}",
  "new_string": "A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than and architecturally independent of all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.\nFrom the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_mean}: mean token-level NLL, capturing the average predictability of the text under the reference model;\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_std}: standard deviation of token-level NLL, measuring the spread of per-token surprise;\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_max}: maximum token-level NLL, capturing the most surprising token;\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_min}: minimum token-level NLL, capturing the most predictable token---as generation depth increases, the floor of token-level surprise drops, reflecting the model's increasing confidence on its own outputs;\n    \\item \\textbf{tail\\_decay}: proportion of tokens whose NLL exceeds the 90th percentile of the reference distribution (computed over $\\mathcal{C}_0$), measuring how heavy-tailed the NLL distribution is relative to human text.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Surface features.}\nTwo features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{TTR}: type-token ratio (number of unique word types divided by total tokens);\n    \\item \\textbf{hapax\\_ratio}: proportion of words appearing exactly once (\\emph{hapax legomena}).\n\\end{itemize}"
}
```

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "23bc5535-0766-4034-9cfb-ad054db2cad7",

  "promptId": "93d04b10-c0e1-4a9a-8980-83118df7611f",
```
